Alexandre Ghiti

4 items
Patches Submitted (4)
Last comment 2026-07-22 [PATCH v2 0/3] mm: bypass swap readahead for zswap 3 patches Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to optimize swap readahead for zswap by splitting the window walk into two passes and reordering it around the distinction between synchronous and asynchronous device reads. The first pass overlaps the synchronous work with disk reads, while the second pass is skipped when the target needs no I/O. This approach helps mixed workloads (zswap + disk) and keeps synchronous-target page-fault latency low.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David Hildenbrand Needs Work LLM
Raised questions about the performance benefits and potential issues with the new approach. Suggested testing and validation before merging.
Signals: NEEDS_TESTING
Alexandre Ghiti Positive LLM
Addressed David's concerns by providing additional testing results and explaining the benefits of the new approach.
Show original comment 
A readahead neighbour on an SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO device (zram) has no
asynchronous I/O to overlap either, exactly like a zswap-resident one.
Extend swap_entry_synchronous() to take the swap device and also return
true for such an entry, so it is deferred to the second pass too. A
same-type neighbour shares the target's device, which cannot be
SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO here (do_swap_page() routes synchronous-device
targets through swapin_sync()), so @si is only non-NULL for cross-device
neighbours, which is exactly where it matters.

This helps a mixed zram+disk workload when the faulting target is on
disk: that fault now overlaps its zram neighbours' reads behind its own
disk I/O, exactly as patch 1 does for zswap neighbours.

Results [vm-scalability mix-rw-seq, 1G zram (SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO, high
prio) + NVMe disk swap (~76% of swap-in from disk), zram=zstd, zswap
off, 4G memcg / 8G region, unthrottled NVMe, n=3]. With zswap off
patches 1-2 are inert, so the delta isolates this patch:

  metric                  mm-new    +patch    delta
  swap-in tput (KB/s)    171226    182046     +6.3%
  major faults           431904    432657     +0.2%
  swap-in pswpin (pages) 1778579   1778473     ~0%
  swap_ra (pages)        1349299   1348382     -0.1%

Signed-off-by: Alexandre Ghiti <alex@ghiti.fr>

2.53.0-Meta
Signals: LGTM
[PATCH v2 1/3] mm: swap: overlap synchronous readahead decompression with disk I/O 3 patches Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the swap readahead process by overlapping synchronous decompression of zswap-resident entries with disk I/O operations. The current implementation serializes CPU work in front of disk reads, introducing unnecessary latency. To address this, the patch splits the window walk into two passes: one issues asynchronous device reads and defers synchronous neighbors, while the second pass reads deferred entries concurrently with ongoing I/O operations. This approach reduces added latency and improves swap-in performance.

[PATCH v2 3/3] mm: swap: treat SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO devices as synchronous in readahead
Show 1 individual patches
  • [2/1] mm: swap: bypass synchronous readahead unless the target needs I/O
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the Linux kernel's swap handling to treat devices marked as SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO (such as zram) as synchronous in readahead operations. This change allows for better overlap of I/O operations between disk and zram, improving performance in mixed workloads. The modification extends the swap_entry_synchronous() function to take into account the swap device and returns true for entries on SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO devices, ensuring that these reads are deferred until the second pass. This patch is part of a series aimed at optimizing swap handling and has been tested with positive results in mixed zram+disk workloads.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-07-18 Last comment 2026-07-20 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] mm: zswap: free cold writeback folios promptly 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm: zswap: free synchronous-IO writeback folios directly Neutral Submitted 2026-07-18Last comment 2026-07-20 Kairui Song Nhat Pham
  • [2/2] mm: zswap: deferred dropbehind free of writeback folios Neutral Submitted 2026-07-18Last comment 2026-07-20 Nhat Pham
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch improves the performance of zswap by freeing cold writeback folios promptly after writeback completion, reducing unnecessary reclaim scans and keeping memory resident for shorter periods. The patch consists of two parts: one for synchronous-IO devices (zram, brd, pmem) that can free the folio directly in the calling context, and another for asynchronous block devices and filesystem-backed swap that allocate the folio off the LRU and defer the free to a workqueue. The results show significant improvements in performance metrics such as page rotations, steals, scans, and build times, with most folios being freed promptly on writeback completion.

I took a quick look through the patches and the implementation seems
too zswap-specific.

I am not super familiar with how the drop-behind mechanism works in
general, but it seems like it's currently intended for file pages and
not swap-backed pages. From a high-level perspective, it seems like we
should add support for swap dropbehind in general first (regardless of
zswap), e.g. by making folio_end_dropbehind() properly handle them,
and then have zswap opt into this?

I guess one of the challenges is that folio_end_dropbehind() only
frees folios in task context while we need to support interrupt
context, but this also doesn't seem like something that should be
handled in zswap.

In theory, there may be a use case for doing dropbehind for swap in
general, right?

Boris Burkov

18 items
Patches Submitted (6)
[PATCH v2 0/4] allocate extent_buffer GFP_NOFAIL with unlocked retry Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in Btrfs where extent_buffer allocations are often done while holding btree locks, leading to stalls and contention. The patches allow for the allocation of extent_buffers with GFP_NOWAIT, then retrying with GFP_NOFS | GFP_NOFAIL outside the critical section if necessary. This approach is analogous to how extent_buffers are read without holding any locks. Although the series does not completely eliminate lock-holding allocations, it significantly reduces their impact on performance.

[PATCH v2 1/4] btrfs: factor init_extent_buffer from __alloc_extent_buffer Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch refactors the initialization of extent buffers in Btrfs by separating it from the allocation process. The new function `init_extent_buffer` initializes an existing extent buffer, while the original `__alloc_extent_buffer` function is now responsible for allocating memory for the buffer. This change allows for preallocation of extent buffer data and simplifies the code paths for different allocation scenarios.

[PATCH v2 2/4] btrfs: add struct btrfs_eb_prealloc Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch introduces a new structure called `struct btrfs_eb_prealloc` to carry the extent buffer and btrfs folio state needed for allocation in preparation for supporting NOFAIL allocations with retries outside the critical section. The existing code is refactored to use this new structure, but no functional changes are made. Empty prealloc structs are wired into callers, and the necessary modifications are made to various files including `ctree.c`, `disk-io.c`, `extent-tree.c`, and others.

[PATCH v2 3/4] btrfs: enable unlocked NOFAIL retry for eb allocations Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch enables unlocked NOFAIL retry for extent buffer (eb) allocations in the Btrfs file system. It introduces a new struct, btrfs_eb_prealloc, to carry allocation information and signals when preallocation is required. If the allocation fails, it returns EAGAIN and allows the caller to retry with the eb, block device, and folios wired through in the prealloc struct. This approach aims to reduce reclaim stalls by allowing allocations that fail due to lack of memory to be retried without holding locks. However, two GFP_NOFS allocations remain under lock and cannot be preallocated.

[PATCH v2 4/4] btrfs: use GFP_NOWAIT for tree block readahead Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the btrfs readahead mechanism to use GFP_NOWAIT instead of the default GFP_KERNEL allocation flag. This change is intended to prevent the readahead from blocking in direct reclaim, which can cause stalls and lock contention. The approach taken is to mark the extent buffer preallocation as supporting nowait allocation, allowing the system to fail the readahead if necessary rather than waiting for memory to become available.

🔥 TODAY [RFC PATCH] btrfs: trigger cow fixup via dirty_folio() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch proposes a new approach to trigger the COW (Copy-On-Write) fixup in Btrfs via the dirty_folio() function, which is used to handle folios that have been improperly dirtied during writeback. The current implementation uses an asynchronous worker to catch and fix such cases, but this patch aims to simplify the process by leveraging the dirty_folio() function. This change addresses a long-standing issue where a dio read may skip dirtying a folio if it is already clean by the end of the I/O operation, leading to potential data corruption. The patch maintains three critical invariants to ensure that each block is written exactly once: any dirty block is covered by delalloc or an ordered extent, any dirty block submitted into an OE will be written back within that OE, and any dirty block already submitted into an OE will not be resubmitted. This change aims to demonstrate a more complex solution to the problem and highlight the issues it must address.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Qu Wenruo ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Qu Wenruo noted that the patch does not properly handle the case where a dio read is in progress and btrfs finishes writing back the folio, causing the dio to re-dirty the folio unnecessarily. They requested that the dirty_folio() call be moved after the folio is unlocked. The reviewer is concerned that the patch's complexity and size are overwhelming due to its rapid development by an LLM, which may lead to issues in review and maintenance. Reviewer Qu Wenruo noted that the call to trigger cow fixup via dirty_folio() is very frequent and redundant, suggesting that it can be merged into btrfs_folio*_set_dirty(), which ensures proper dirtying and cancellation of the fixup. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested simplifying the dirty_folio() check by only verifying if the fixup bit is not set, as this indicates that the folio has gone through regular write preparation and should have EXTENT_DELALLOC. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested replacing the use of btrfs_is_subpage() and related functions from subpage.c with a custom helper function, btrfs_folio_test_fixup(), to improve code readability. The reviewer noted that if a folio has already been dirtied properly, calling ->dirty_folio() may trigger a false alert because there might be no ordered extent (OE) created yet for the folio, violating one of the critical invariants. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested simplifying the code by skipping ordered extent (OE) lookup and letting the final submission path handle it, citing a recent patch that achieves similar functionality at submission time. Reviewer Qu Wenruo expressed concern about the variable naming in the patch, stating that it appears to be generated by a Large Language Model (LLM) and may require additional explanation for non-native speakers. Reviewer Qu Wenruo questioned whether triggering cow fixup via dirty_folio() is necessary because the folio has not yet been dirtied, and expressed uncertainty about the need for this patch. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested using btrfs_folio_clear_fixup() instead of the proposed fix to trigger cow fixup via dirty_folio(), indicating that their preferred approach is more helpful. Reviewer Qu Wenruo expressed frustration with the LLM's comments and suggested deleting them by default, implying that they are unnecessary or unhelpful. The reviewer, Qu Wenruo, pointed out that the patch can lead to over-reservation of space for already properly dirtied ranges, making it impossible to release reserved space without clearing qgroup flags. This issue is difficult to expose through tests because dirty folios are hard to trigger. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested using a proper btrfs_folio_clear_fixup() function to handle the loop, passing in the folio range instead of relying on dirty_folio(). Reviewer Qu Wenruo pointed out that the patch's approach to handling dirty folios via dirty_folio() is unnecessary, as regular btrfs_folio_clear_dirty() can handle all these cases and there's no need for a special subpage branch. Reviewer Qu Wenruo questioned the necessity of using dirty_folio() when folio_test_dirty() would be sufficient, specifically pointing out that the subpage part does not have this check. Reviewer Qu Wenruo noted that calling folio_mark_dirty() would lead to a recursive call to ->dirty_folio(), and instead implemented a local version of filemap_mark_dirty() to set the fixup bits. The reviewer noted that the patch may not fully address the COW fixup issue, suggesting that additional checks are needed to ensure that the last fixup bit is cleared and the folio fixup flag is reset. Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested that the btrfs_page_ops implementation should handle all fixup helpers, rather than relying on a separate dirty_folio() function. Reviewer Qu Wenruo noted that since btrfs_folio_set_dirty() does not call folio_mark_dirty(), the fixup bit is not actually being set, and suggested removing the dirty_folio() call to skip setting the fixup bit.

Boris Burkov (author) ↳ replying to Qu Wenruo Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that some invariants were not properly trimmed from the LLM-generated code and agreed to rework them. Author acknowledged that the delalloc lookup is essential for distinguishing between re-dirtying and true fixups, but is considering simplifying the code by removing the possibility of re-dirtying in typical cases. Author acknowledged that the code to check if ordered extent is present is unnecessary and agreed to simplify the logic by removing or hiding it, depending on the configuration. The author acknowledged a minor issue with technical terminology and promised to correct it. Author acknowledged a concern about the complexity of the proposed fixup worker and its potential for 'word salad' code, but did not indicate plans to revise or restructure the patch. Author acknowledged a potential issue where aops->dirty_folio() is called without locking the folio, but believes the current implementation's strategy of reserving the whole range and then freeing parts that don't get 'funded' should handle cancellations correctly. However, they acknowledge it's an ugly solution and consider an alternative approach with locking to be appealing but potentially flawed due to a TOCTOU race. Author acknowledges that avoiding the 'bfs->reserving_dirty' approach is a good idea and seems open to revising their patch, but doesn't explicitly state they will make changes. Author agrees that using dirty_folio() is an improvement, acknowledges current implementation as a 'stupid way' that introduces unnecessary risk, but does not indicate plans to revise the patch.

100% agreed, I tried to trim them down and rewrite them in human but
missed a bunch in the rush.

---

the delalloc lookup is essential for bs == folio_size, to tell apart
the case where you are submitting a folio that got redirtied behind you
(don't skip) and the case where it is a true fixup.

Thinking about it more, though, that re-dirty shouldn't really be
possible at least in typical cases, so maybe I can figure out how to
remove this.

I think for subpage it probably doesn't matter, because those should not
have fixup in their bitmaps.

---

Agreed. This is vestigial from a long period where ordered was really
important before I tightened the logic and left it as an assertion, but
that is a big overkill for not DEBUG at least. I'll either remove it or
hide it behind CONFIG_BTRFS_DEBUG.

---

Yes, I hate when they do that. This one was just natural enough to me
that I let it go, but it is totally pointless jargon. I'll fix it.

---

yup, haha missed this one. You would have loved the ones that got
deleted :D

it was like scrolls full of word salad at every if(...)

---

I think this approach here seems correct to me, except for maybe a
really mean case which calls aops->dirty_folio() without locking the folio
(not the case for dio read at least).

Won't any real reservation will cancel the fixup bits under the lock, so
the way this implementation tries to handle it is to reserve the whole
range then free the parts that don't end up getting in the "funded"
bitmap (also a bad LLM name...). This is checking the fixup bitmap under
the lock, so should see correct cancellations. Do you see an obvious
problem with that strategy, besides that it is kind of ugly and annoying
to handle the 'out:' path. Which feels similarly painful to a lot of our
other delalloc/qgroup_rsv unwinding logic with data_reserveds on OE error
or whatnot.

I think the clearer option of "take the lock, observe the real set,
release, then reserve, then take the lock and do all the work" sounds
appealing but almost certainly fails to some simple TOCTOU race.

---

You mean to avoid the ugly bfs->reserving_dirty thing?
That does sound appealing...

---

yes, that seems like an obvious improvement. I think we get away with it
in a super stupid way that writepage_fixup will walk the bitmap and see
all clear and clear it there. Complete waste and adds risk for no
reason.

Show original comment 

\u5728 2026/7/24 03:58, Boris Burkov \u5199\u9053:
> On Thu, Jul 23, 2026 at 04:58:57AM +0200, David Sterba wrote:
>> On Tue, Jul 21, 2026 at 03:42:11PM -0700, Boris Burkov wrote:
>>> >From sampled fleet data measuring lock holders that go into direct
>>> reclaim with a waiter present when they eventually unlock, we have
>>> observed ~15% of those are btrfs extent_buffer allocations in
>>> btrfs_search_slot() done while holding btree locks. This is the single
>>> largest category. Additionally, a large source of hung_task timeouts is
>>> both btree waiters and direct reclaiming btree allocations, which
>>> further motivates the desire to drive down this source of stalls and
>>> contention.
>>>
>>> The aim of this series is to allow us to allocate the extent_buffer,
>>> btrfs_folio_state, and the extent_buffer folios with GFP_NOWAIT then
>>> fallback with EAGAIN to outside the critical section to retry with
>>> GFP_NOFS | GFP_NOFAIL without any locks held.
>>>
>>> This is analogous to how we must drop locks to read an extent_buffer and
>>> then EAGAIN.
>>>
>>> The series does not manage to completely eliminate allocations from this
>>> lock holding path, as we also allocate inside xarray functions for the
>>> extent_buffer xarray and the btree_inode mapping xarray, the latter of
>>> which is done via filemap_add_folio() with no reserve type API. Luckily,
>>> those particular allocations are small cached slab allocations and have
>>> nearly no contribution to the production reclaim fueled contention.
>>>
>>> Changelog:
>>> v2:
>>> - rebase
>>> - dropped the inhibit array allocation patch
>>
>> Reviewed-by: David Sterba <dsterba@suse.com>
>>
>> The logic makes sense to avoid the stalls. There's no sashiko review
>> because the patches did not apply. You may want to resend a rebased
>> version to see if I have missed something, there are minor typos or
>> style things but that can be fixed during merge.
>

Re: [PATCH/RFC] btrfs: fix folio lock leak in writepage_delalloc() for folios dirtied behind btrfs' back Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
🔥 TODAY Re: [RFC PATCH] btrfs: trigger cow fixup via dirty_folio() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the Btrfs file system to trigger cow (copy-on-write) fixup via dirty_folio(). The change aims to improve handling of delalloc lookup and reservation in the presence of subpage folios, which are not expected to have fixup bits. It also removes vestigial code related to ordered operations and reworks the implementation to handle potential TOCTOU (time-of-check-to-time-of-use) races. The patch introduces a new approach to reserve the whole range and then free parts that don't end up in the 'funded' bitmap, which is considered an improvement over the previous implementation.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Qu Wenruo ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Qu Wenruo suggested using folio->private to add an extra bit indicating whether a folio is properly dirtied, similar to the subpage dirty bit for bs == folio size case. Reviewer Qu Wenruo pointed out the need for a local function, btrfs_folio_mark_dirty_prepared(), which would copy folio_mark_dirty() but call filemap_dirty_folio() instead of aops->dirty_folio(). They expressed uncertainty about whether MM will be happy with this change.

> I think this placement makes sense, but I also wouldn't mind just having

Furthermore, I feel like this is actually quite redundant with the whole
EBUSY pivot logic.

So for random reclaim writeback, we walk the ebs in index order and skip
any not in the active bg if the active bg has dirty ebs. Then we reach
the active bg and flush it, submitting everything. Subsequent tries to
writeback the non-active will now wait on that submitted IO and
ultimately win and get to become active, and do more submission.

But we could save the bother and make things more direct and simple if
we just had btree_writepages() always write out the active bg first then
the rest. I don't know if we could fully delete the EBUSY, though, maybe
there is some racy case where it would still happen a bit, even if
mostly it didn't?

> I think fewer "tricks" for zoned writeback and more "it falls out
> correctly from a generic form" would make it easier to maintain in the
> long run. Obviously there are real, important differences so this kind
> of thing is never free...
> 
> Thanks,
> Boris

[RFC PATCH 0/8] dax/kmem: support for private NUMA nodes 8 patches
Show 8 individual patches
  • [1/8] dax/kmem: add dax_file= to expose an anonymous-fault char device
  • [2/8] dax/kmem: add private= to online memory as an N_MEMORY_PRIVATE node
  • [3/8] dax/kmem: add the reclaim opt-in (CAP_RECLAIM) for private nodes
  • [4/8] dax/kmem: add the user_numa opt-in (CAP_USER_NUMA) for private nodes
  • [5/8] dax/kmem: add the hotunplug opt-in (CAP_HOTUNPLUG) for private nodes
  • [6/8] dax/kmem: add the demotion and numa_balancing opt-ins for private nodes
  • [7/8] dax/kmem: add the ltpin opt-in (CAP_LTPIN) for private nodes
  • [8/8] dax/kmem: add dax/adistance to make private node adistance configurable
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series adds support for private NUMA nodes to the dax/kmem driver, allowing for more fine-grained control over memory allocation and management. The patches introduce new uapi interfaces for controlling private node settings, including options for reclaiming memory, enabling user-level NUMA policies, and configuring adistance for private nodes. The goal is to enable testing of private node behaviors and determine whether some of these features are useful for coherent memory devices. The series includes 8 patches that modify the dax/kmem driver to expose new interfaces and capabilities.

[PATCH v5 36/36] KVM: selftests: add a guest_memfd FLAG_BIND_NODE test Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch adds a standalone test to verify the functionality of GUEST_MEMFD_FLAG_BIND_NODE, which allows a guest_memfd to be bound to a specific NUMA node. The test creates a guest_memfd bound to a given node, maps and faults it from the host, and checks that every folio landed on the requested node using move_pages(2). It also covers rejection paths for invalid inputs such as nonzero pad, out-of-range nodes, and missing flags. The test requires CONFIG_NUMA and uses a default (non-protected) VM to allow the host to fault the fd and observe placement.

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch introduces the concept of 'Private Memory Nodes', which are isolated by default and can be opted into specific services such as page allocation and OOM killing. The goal is to flip the current dynamic where NUMA-hosted memory is accessible by default, and instead isolate it and then opt-in to specific services that the device says is safe. This allows for more secure and efficient use of accelerators, special memory devices, network devices, and other types of memory. The patch reduces complexity and introduces a dedicated zonelist for private nodes, making it easier to manage and control memory access.

Gregory Price (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the find_next_best_node() function, which picks the next-closest node for a fallback list from the full N_MEMORY set. The author refactored it into find_next_best_node_in(), which takes an explicit candidates nodemask, enabling building fallback lists with non-N_MEMORY candidates. No functional change is made, as every caller still selects from N_MEMORY.
Show original comment 
find_next_best_node() picks the next-closest node for a fallback list
from the full N_MEMORY set. Refactor it into find_next_best_node_in(),
which takes an explicit candidates nodemask.

This enables building fallback lists with non-N_MEMORY candidates.

No functional change: every caller still selects from N_MEMORY.

Gregory Price (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the complexity of build_zonelists() by extracting per-node fallback-list construction into a new function, build_node_zonelist(), which builds zonelists from candidate nodemasks instead of just the default N_MEMORY node state list. This change has no functional impact and is intended to simplify the code.
Show original comment 
Extract per-node fallback-list construction into build_node_zonelist().

This lets use build zonelists from candidate nodemasks instead of just
the default N_MEMORY node state list.

No functional change: build_zonelists() builds the same FALLBACK list over
N_MEMORY with node_load updates as before.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

Signals: clarification, no clear resolution signal
Gregory Price (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the lack of explicit alloc_flags in bulk and folio allocation functions, explaining that they will add __alloc_pages_bulk_noprof() and modify existing functions to take an alloc_flags parameter, matching the behavior of __alloc_pages_noprof(). The changes are non-functional and only affect internal flag-carrying primitives.
Show original comment 
__alloc_pages_noprof() takes an explicit alloc_flags, but the bulk and
folio entry points do not, so callers cannot select allocator behaviour
(e.g. an alternate zonelist) through them.

Thread alloc_flags through both, matching __alloc_pages_noprof(), and
keep the flag-carrying primitives mm-internal (page_alloc.h) so the
public gfp.h wrappers stay flag-free:

 - add __alloc_pages_bulk_noprof(gfp, alloc_flags, ...) in page_alloc.h
   alloc_pages_bulk_noprof() becomes a wrapper passing ALLOC_DEFAULT

 - give __folio_alloc_noprof() an alloc_flags parameter and moves
   __folio_alloc_node_noprof() moves into page_alloc.c
   __folio_alloc_noprof() is no longer exported

No functional change: every caller passes ALLOC_DEFAULT.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Gregory Price (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about mutual exclusivity between N_MEMORY and N_MEMORY_PRIVATE states by explaining that they are mutually exclusive, adding checks to ensure this in node_private_register(), and clarifying the behavior of node_state() and helpers when CONFIG_NUMA is not set. A fix is planned for v2.
Show original comment 
Some devices want to hotplug their memory onto a node, but not have it
exposed as "general purpose".  The intent is to re-use portions of mm/
services for management without exposing it for general consumption.

N_MEMORY nodes are intended to contain general System RAM, so they
are unsuited for this purpose.

Create N_MEMORY_PRIVATE for memory nodes whose memory is not intended
for general consumption.

N_MEMORY and N_MEMORY_PRIVATE are mutually exclusive states.

This commit adds basic infrastructure for N_MEMORY_PRIVATE nodes:

  - struct node_private:
      Per-node container stored in NODE_DATA(nid) storing the owner.

  - folio/page_is_private_node():
       true if it resides on a private node

  - folio/page_is_private_managed():
      common predicate for checking private node or zone device
      Used to combine common filtering locations.

  - Registration API: node_private_register()/unregister()

  - sysfs attribute exposing N_MEMORY_PRIVATE node state.

  - fix node_state() and helpers to return false for N_MEMORY_PRIVATE
    when !CONFIG_NUMA (mutually exclusive w/ N_MEMORY)

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

 			   (void *)&nid, register_mem_block_under_node_hotplug);
 	return;
 }
+
+static DEFINE_MUTEX(node_private_lock);
+
+/**
+ * node_private_register - Register a private node
+ * @nid: Node identifier
+ * @np: The node_private structure (driver-allocated, driver-owned)
+ *
+ * Register an owner for a private node. If an owner is already registered
+ * (different np), return -EBUSY.
+ *
+ * Re-registration with the same np is allowed.
+ *
+ * The caller owns the node_private memory and must ensure it remains valid
+ * until after node_private_unregister() returns.
+ *
+ * Returns 0 on success, negative errno on failure.
+ */
+int node_private_register(int nid, struct node_private *np)
+{
+	struct node_private *existing;
+	pg_data_t *pgdat;
+	int ret = 0;
+
+	if (!np || !node_possible(nid))
+		return -EINVAL;
+
+	mutex_lock(&node_private_lock);
+	mem_hotplug_begin();
+
+	/* N_MEMORY_PRIVATE and N_MEMORY are mutually exclusive */
+	if (node_state(nid, N_MEMORY)) {
+		ret = -EBUSY;
+		goto out;
+	}
+
+	pgdat = NODE_DATA(nid);
+	existing = rcu_dereference_protected(pgdat->node_private,
+					     lockdep_is_held(&node_private_lock));
+
+	/* If it exists, restrict to single owner */
+	if (existing) {
+		if (existing != np)
+			ret = -EBUSY;
+		goto out;
+	}
+
+	rcu_assign_pointer(pgdat->node_private, np);
+out:
+	mem_hotplug_done();
+	mutex_unlock(&node_private_lock);
+	return ret;
+}
+EXPORT_SYMBOL_GPL(node_private_register);
+
+/**
+ * node_private_unregister - Unregister a private node
+ * @nid: Node identifier
+ *
+ * Unregister the driver from a private node.
+ *
+ * Only succeeds if all memory has been offlined (N_MEMORY_PRIVATE cleared).
+ *
+ * N_MEMORY_PRIVATE state is cleared by offline_pages() when the last
+ * memory is offlined, not by this function.
+ *
+ * After this returns, the node_private pointer is no longer visible and
+ * an RCU grace period has elapsed, so the driver may free its context.
+ *
+ * Return: 0 if unregistered, -EBUSY if N_MEMORY_PRIVATE is still set.
+ */
+int node_private_unregister(int nid)
+{
+	struct node_private *np;
+	pg_data_t *pgdat;
+
+	if (!node_possible(nid))
+		return 0;
+
+	mutex_lock(&node_private_lock);
+	mem_hotplug_begin();
+
+	pgdat = NODE_DATA(nid);
+	np = rcu_dereference_protected(pgdat->node_private,
+				       lockdep_is_held(&node_private_lock));
+	if (!np) {
+		mem_hotplug_done();
+		mutex_unlock(&node_private_lock);
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	/*
+	 * Only unregister if N_MEMORY_PRIVATE is cleared (only occurs when
+	 * the last memory block is offlined in offline_pages())
+	 */
+	if (node_is_private(nid)) {
+		mem_hotplug_done();
+		mutex_unlock(&node_private_lock);
+		return -EBUSY;
+	}
+
+	rcu_assign_pointer(pgdat->node_private, NULL);
+
+	mem_hotplug_done();
+	mutex_unlock(&node_private_lock);
+
+	synchronize_rcu();
+	return 0;
+}
+EXPORT_SYMBOL_GPL(node_private_unregister);
 #endif /* CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTPLUG */

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a concern, planned a fix
Gregory Price (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about unintentional allocations reaching N_MEMORY_PRIVATE nodes by making these nodes invisible to ordinary allocation and adding explicit private-node alloc() functions, but noted that private-node allocations can still fall back to non-private nodes.
Show original comment 
N_MEMORY_PRIVATE nodes must be invisible to ordinary allocation,
no allocations may reach one unless explicitly called for.

Make this isolation structural in the zonelists: N_MEMORY_PRIVATE
nodes are excluded from FALLBACK and NOFALLBACK entirely, making
them effectively invisible to all normal callers of page_alloc.

Add ZONELIST_PRIVATE, which spans (N_MEMORY | N_MEMORY_PRIVATE).
When !CONFIG_NUMA, it aliases ZONELIST_FALLBACK and compiles out.

Add ZONELIST_PRIVATE_NOFALLBACK as the __GFP_THISNODE target for
private node allocations.

Zonelist selection rides the allocator's alloc_flags.  Add
ALLOC_ZONELIST_PRIVATE and resolve it in select_zonelist(), which
prepare_alloc_pages() applies from ac->alloc_flags; the default
(ALLOC_DEFAULT) keeps node_zonelist()'s gfp-based selection.

Add explicit private-node alloc() functions to prevent open-coding
(both ride ALLOC_ZONELIST_PRIVATE through the alloc_flags-carrying
entry points):
   - alloc_pages_node_private_noprof()
   - folio_alloc_node_private_noprof()

alloc_contig_range adds an explicit node_is_private() guard because
it dervices its target zones from PFNs rather than zonelists. All
in-tree callers use N_MEMORY ranges, but the check prevents future
callers from violating node isolation.

Important note:  By design this zonelist isolates N_MEMORY_PRIVATE
from unintentional allocations, but does NOT isolate private-node
allocations from falling back to non-private nodes.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

+/*
+ * select_zonelist - resolve the zonelist for an allocation
+ *
+ * The default matches node_zonelist(); ALLOC_ZONELIST_PRIVATE selects the
+ * private-node family so an allocation may reach an N_MEMORY_PRIVATE node.
+ */
+static inline struct zonelist *
+select_zonelist(int nid, gfp_t gfp, unsigned int alloc_flags)
+{
+#ifdef CONFIG_NUMA
+	if (alloc_flags & ALLOC_ZONELIST_PRIVATE) {
+		int idx = ZONELIST_PRIVATE + !!(gfp & __GFP_THISNODE);
+
+		return &NODE_DATA(nid)->node_zonelists[idx];
+	}
+#endif
+	return node_zonelist(nid, gfp);
+}

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a technical issue, provided additional context
Gregory Price (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern that cpuset operations should not partition private nodes via cpuset.mem and instead treat them as globally accessible resources. The author explained that isolating by cpuset offers no functionality and creates issues on rebind when a cpuset loses access to a node, generating migrations that may not be supported. They also stated that silent background operations on these nodes due to cpuset.mem membership changing should be avoided. A fix is planned for the next version.
Show original comment 
N_MEMORY_PRIVATE node access is gated by per-node capabilities and
the zonelist.  Isolating by cpuset offers no functionality and
creates issues on rebind when a cpuset loses access to that node
(in particular: it generates migrations that may not be supported).

Do not partition private nodes via cpuset.mem, instead treat them
as globally accessible resources.  Do not engage in silent background
operations on these nodes due to cpuset.mem membership changing.

The result: cpuset operations checking for node validity always
allow N_MEMORY_PRIVATE nodes.

On hot-unplug, we still need to rebind memory policies if the
private node has left N_MEMORY_PRIVATE (i.e. no more memory).

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
---

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a concern, explained reasoning
Gregory Price (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about migrating private node memory without coordination by changing the hotunplug attempt to fail instead of spinning forever on a failed migration attempt, and requiring the owner to drain the folios safely before allowing hotunplug to proceed.
Show original comment 
By default, device-backed private node memory may not be safe to
migrate without coordination.

Do not attempt to hotunplug private node memory, instead fail the
hotunplug attempt, and require the owner to drain the folios
safely before allowing hotunplug to proceed.

Unlike transient / longterm pins causing failures, this is not a
transient condition - so we can break out of the hotunplug attempt
entirely instead of spinning forever on a failed migration attempt.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
---

View on lore ↗
Signals: no clear resolution signal, author explains reasoning
Gregory Price (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about private nodes being moved by mempolicy-driven migration, explaining that they are already kept out of policy nodemasks and adding a filter to skip private-node folios during migration. A fix is planned for the next version.
Show original comment 
Private nodes are already kept out of policy nodemasks (only N_MEMORY
nodes are allowed), but an mbind(MPOL_MF_MOVE) walk can still encounter a
private-node folio in the range.  Skip such folios so mempolicy-driven
migration never moves private-node memory.

We already do this for ZONE_DEVICE folios - use the same filter points
for ZONE_DEVICE (with an additional point for large pages).

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

Re: [RFC PATCH 0/8] dax/kmem: support for private NUMA nodes Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-07-22 Re: [PATCH v5 14/36] mm/damon: skip private node memory in DAMON migration and pageout Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies DAMON (Dynamic Access Monitor) to skip memory from private nodes during migration and pageout operations. This change ensures that user-controlled MADV commands for private node folios behave similarly to ZONE_DEVICE folios, which are already handled correctly by DAMON. The patch addresses a potential issue where DAMON might not handle zone device pages properly.

Initially proposed zonelist isolation to prevent implicit opt-ins, but later walked back this approach due to complexity and potential issues with mempolicy and demotion. Now suggests including any CAP_USER_NUMA node in cpuset.mems for partitioning.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Jul 22, 2026 at 11:40:06AM -0400, Gregory Price wrote:
> On Wed, Jul 22, 2026 at 10:20:00PM +0800, Richard Cheng wrote:

> > global, unbypasssable thing in MM design, but now what you
> > are trying to do is to seperate the whole global list into several
> > parts ?
> > 
> > Can you explain why in current design you don't consider to support
> > something like ZONELIST_PRIVATE[n]={0, .. ,n-1} ? that's my imagination
> > of what a global zonelist should look like, no matter private or non.
> > 
> 
> First let me say that there's nothing that prevents us from doing this,
> and we *could* make this the default case - but this decision was
> intentional by me.
> 

You caught me on something here - I am wrong about my own code.

I described to you how I originally implemented zonelist isolation,
which I walked back when I was testing mempolicy and demotion.

The current code and the comments in the code are correct, we get
the following zonelists.

N_MEMORY : 0,1
N_MEMORY_PRIVATE: 2,3

ZONELIST_PRIVATE[0]     = {0,1,2,3}
ZONELIST_PRIVATE[1]     = {1,0,2,3}
ZONELIST_PRIVATE[2]     = {2,0,1,3}
ZONELIST_PRIVATE[3]     = {3,0,1,2}

Meaning everything except for MPOL_F_RELATIVE_NODES works (because
of cpuset isolation, but more on this in a moment).


So what I shipped here is what you are describing.

Looking back at my notes - I realized a few things that lead me to
walk back that isolation:

1) mempolicy makes more sense this way.
2) reclaim/demotion is easier to implement without isolation.
3) The individual service checks and nodelists handle the rest.
4) It overally just makes more sense.  If something breaks isolation
   it's because there's a bug, not because of a structural issue.

For example in demotion we do this:

demote_folio_list():
    /* Get demotion targets, find the best one, and demote */
    node_get_allowed_targets(pgdat, &allowed_mask);
    target_nid = next_demotion_node(pgdat->node_id, &allowed_mask);
    migrate_pages(demote_folios, alloc_demote_folio, ...);

Where the first step already filters on CAP_DEMOTION.
The result is still clean - no special iterators needed.

What I realized from your questions here is that since i walked back the
zonelist isolation, I think i can actually include any CAP_USER_NUMA
node in cpuset.mems and allow parititioning fairly trivially.

I think this might be the last majority complexity falling out.

Thank you for the questions - this has helped.

~Gregory

Last comment 2026-07-23 Re: [PATCH] mm: mempolicy: fix automatic numa balancing for shmem Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a problem with automatic NUMA balancing for shared memory (shmem) by fixing the default policy used when no explicit policy is set. The issue arises from silently inherited policies on boot, which can be confusing for users and lead to unexpected behavior. To resolve this, the patch proposes setting a default global policy that includes MPOL_LOCAL and additional flags to ensure consistent NUMA balancing.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Johannes Weiner ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Johannes Weiner pointed out that the documentation does not clearly state the default global policy for numa balancing, specifically mentioning that MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON are internal flags and should not be silently inherited on boot. He suggested adding a doc addition to clarify this.
Show original comment 
Hm, where do you see "Instead of just MPOL_LOCAL"?

Documentation/filesystems/tmpfs.rst says it defaults to "default" and
points me to set_mempolicy(2). That in turn in general makes little
mention of numa balancing and doesn't mention MPOL_F_MOF/MPOL_F_MORON.
In fact these are documented as internal flags.

The numa_balancing entry in
Documentation/admin-guide/sysctl/kernel.rst however DOES say plainly
that enabling this feature will sample and migrate mapped process
memory to where it's used, and that this has a performance penalty.

It seems there is a general lack of documented interactions between
user-requested policies and the system-wide numa-balancing behavior.
View on lore ↗
Signals: lack of documentation, silent implementation detail
Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING is an internal flag, essentially a shorthand for (F_MOF | F_MORON), and explained its usage as 'internal' and generated by passing this flag. Author acknowledged Johannes' feedback about the silent implementation detail of MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON being inherited on boot, and suggested setting MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING in default/preferred policies to make it clear that numa balancing is enabled by default. The author proposes documenting this behavior.
Show original comment 
They're "internal" and generated by passing MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING...
which is basically shorthand for "use (F_MOF | F_MORON)".  There is
no other user of MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING.

The internal/external state of uapi/linux/mempolicy.h is frustrating
to say the last.

---

Agreed, that's basically what i'm pointing out.

This is what most think they're getting:

    static struct mempolicy default_policy = {
        .refcnt = ATOMIC_INIT(1), /* never free it */
        .mode = MPOL_LOCAL,
    };

This is what they're actually getting:

    struct mempolicy *get_task_policy(struct task_struct *p)
    {
        ...
                pol = &preferred_node_policy[node];
                /* preferred_node_policy is not initialised early in boot */
                if (pol->mode)
                        return pol;
    }

    void __init numa_policy_init(void)
    {
    ...
            for_each_node(nid) {
                preferred_node_policy[nid] = (struct mempolicy) {
                    .refcnt = ATOMIC_INIT(1),
                    .mode = MPOL_PREFERRED,
                    .flags = MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON,
                    .nodes = nodemask_of_node(nid),
            };
        }
    }

So the default global mempolicy is effectively:

    (MPOL_PREFERRED | (MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON)

which is just MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING without MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING.

The user can't actually see that - because the MOF/MORON flags get
stripped out when it's queried (because they're internal only).

Maybe we should just set MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING in the default/preferred
policies and document that the global preferred policy default-enables
MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING - and that if a user (KVM) doesn't want to be
affected by numa balancing it needs to set a policy to disable it.

~Gregory
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged technical detail, explained complexity, acknowledged a fix is needed
Gregory Price (author) Positive LLM

Show original comment 
Walking this all back:

  - MPOL_DEFAULT is the default policy
  - numa_balancing=on means enable NUMA_BALANCING on the defaul policy
  - shmem was broken and did not respect this

Johannes' fix is correct, and there are no additional changes required.

Users enabling numa balancing while running KVM with shared regions are
not getting what they're asking for presently - that is a bug.

They should get the performance hit they're asking for (and then either
turn off numa balancing or set an explicit mempolicy for those tasks /
memfd's).

~Gregory

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a concern about handling ENOMEM errors in btrfs_insert_dir_item() without causing an abort, specifically focusing on whether this approach will lead to further ENOMEM errors in other functions like btrfs_orphan_add(). The patch aims to provide more concrete evidence that this handling strategy is effective and worth the added complexity.

8 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Boris Burkov questioned whether the patch addresses a real-world issue, citing concerns that allocations involved in btrfs_orphan_add() might succeed while those for delayed_node fail due to their similar size and potential memory requirements under reclaim conditions.
Show original comment 
I feel like the crux of this series to me is whether you have practical
conditions where the allocation of the delayed_node is failing, but the
allocations involved in btrfs_orphan_add() succeed. It allocates a
btrfs_path and has to walk the btree which might have to read the node
at every level which might need to allocate 16k extent buffers and
extent buffer objects and xarray storage for each one. For size
reference, on my build (maybe debug..?) a delayed_node is 552 bytes,
while a btrfs_path is 112 and an extent_buffer is 432. So they are
pretty similar in size (not to mention the 16k of node file backed
memory we are sort of likely to have to allocate if we are under
reclaim)

Were you able to reproduce this issue and help in practice or is this a
theoretical / structural improvement?

With that said, all the prealloc wiring looks good to me in general, and
it seems to be a pretty clean win for the "name exists" case in the next
patch.

Thanks,
Boris

Reviewer Jeff Layton expressed concerns about the practicality of reproducing the ENOMEM issue in btrfs_insert_dir_item() under heavy memory pressure, but noted that there are records of filesystem aborts where this patch would have prevented them. Last comment 2026-07-22 Re: [syzbot] [nfs?] INFO: task hung in nfsd_nl_cache_flush_doit Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses an issue where the nfsd_nl_cache_flush_doit function hangs, potentially causing a lockup warning. The problem is likely due to a large number of listeners, which can cause the registration process to take too long. To solve this, the patch proposes implementing a more structural solution, possibly involving asynchronous processing of registration requests or keeping a queue of requests and processing them in order from a dedicated thread.

We already throw a printk() when registration fails for v2/3. IIRC,
it's not considered fatal, so we just keep going anyway if that
happens.

My main concern with doing it async would be getting reg/unreg requests
out of order and leaving rpcbind with a broken table, but I think we
could probably do something like keep a queue of requests and process
them in order from a dedicated thread or by driving async RPC
submissions serially from callbacks.

svc_register() prints a warning, then gives up on other
versions/programs.
if svc_setup_socket() get an error from svc_register() it aborts, which
ripples back to svc_addsock() aborting, so the listener doesn't get added.

Then if no listeners got registered, nfsd refuses to start.

It would likely make sense for the registration to happen in a separate
thread and to retry on failure following a normal rpc fail/retry
pattern.

Then if the admin restarts rpcbind, it will start working without
further intervention.

---

A single-threaded work-queue (per net-namespace) seems like a good
solution.  Each listener has a work_item that is used for registration
and de-registration.  We sync-cancel it before re-using it.

NeilBrown

Ongoing Submitted 2026-07-16 Last comment 2026-07-21 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v6 0/6] fuse: add io-uring buffer pools and zero-copy 6 patches
Show 6 individual patches
  • [1/6] fuse: decouple fuse_ring creation from ent registration Neutral Submitted 2026-07-16Last comment 2026-07-21 Miklos Szeredi Joanne Koong (author)
  • [2/6] fuse: add FUSE_IO_URING_CMD_ADD_QUEUE Submitted 2026-07-16
  • [3/6] fuse: add io-uring buffer pools Submitted 2026-07-16
  • [4/6] fuse: support registered buffer pools in io-uring Submitted 2026-07-16
  • [5/6] fuse: add zero-copy over io-uring Submitted 2026-07-16
  • [6/6] docs: fuse: document io-uring buffer pool and zero-copy uapi Submitted 2026-07-16
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series adds support for io-uring buffer pools to the FUSE file system, allowing servers to pass in a contiguous chunk of memory that the kernel can use to service requests. This decouples the request and payload buffers, reducing memory usage requirements and enabling optimizations for assigning payload memory. Additionally, the series introduces zero-copy functionality, which allows servers with CAP_SYS_ADMIN privileges to directly access client pages or page cache folios without copying data through an intermediary buffer. The zero-copy feature requires using buffer pools and has a dependency on io-uring registered bvec changes. The patch series includes benchmark results showing significant throughput improvements for direct-I/O operations, particularly when backing I/O is not the bottleneck.

  • [20/20] Documentation: iomap: update docs to reflect iomap_iter_next model
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series converts the iomap code to use an iterator model, replacing the current begin/end pair of callbacks with a single next callback. This change allows for better performance by enabling inlining and devirtualization of the iteration loop, as well as providing more flexibility for future callers to express custom logic in the IO path. The patches are broken down into 20 parts, including refactoring existing code, converting each filesystem, removing legacy paths, and updating documentation.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Joanne Koong (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the iomap_iter() function's return semantics, acknowledged that it needs to zero iter.status on error paths, and confirmed no functional change is intended.
Show original comment 
In preparation for changing iomap to use an in-iter (->iomap_next())
model, move the iomap_iter() logic out into the new iomap_iter_next()
helper function.

iomap_iter_next() is added as an inlined helper so it can be called
directly by ->iomap_next() implementations where the begin()/end()
callbacks can be direct calls.

The DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT() and DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT_END() macros are
also provided to generate the boilerplate ->iomap_next() wrapper
functions that simply forward to iomap_iter_next() with the appropriate
begin/end callbacks. DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT() is for the common case
where there is no end() callback. DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT_END() is for
the case where there is an explicit end() callback.

No functional change intended. The only code-level difference is that on
the iomap_end() error path (ret < 0 && !advanced), the old code returned
with iter.status left as the caller's last value whereas the new code
zeroes it, but this is not observable in practice as there are no in-tree
callers that read iter.status after the iteration loop.

Signed-off-by: Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com>
---

+/**
+ * iomap_iter - iterate over ranges in a file
+ * @iter: iteration structure
+ * @ops: iomap ops provided by the filesystem
+ *
+ * Iterate over filesystem-provided space mappings for the provided file range.
+ *
+ * This function handles cleanup of resources acquired for iteration when the
+ * filesystem indicates there are no more space mappings, which means that this
+ * function must be called in a loop that continues as long it returns a
+ * positive value.  If 0 or a negative value is returned, the caller must not

+	iter->status = 0;
+	if (ret > 0)
+		iomap_iter_done(iter);
+
+	return ret;

+int iomap_iter_continue(const struct iomap_iter *iter, struct iomap *iomap,
+		struct iomap *srcmap, int ret);

+/**
+ * iomap_iter_next - finish the previous mapping and produce the next one
+ * @iter: iteration structure
+ * @iomap: mapping to finish and then repopulate
+ * @srcmap: source mapping to finish and then repopulate
+ * @begin: callback that produces a mapping for the current position
+ * @end: optional callback that finishes the previous mapping, or NULL
+ *
+ * Inline helper that implements the common body of an ->iomap_next()
+ * callback: it finishes the previous mapping via @end (if present), decides
+ * via iomap_iter_continue() whether to keep going, and obtains the next
+ * mapping via @begin.
+ *
+ * This helper is marked __always_inline so that when a caller passes
+ * compile-time-constant @begin and @end callbacks, the compiler can call them
+ * directly, avoiding the indirect-call overhead.
+ *
+ * Returns 1 to continue iterating, 0 once the range is fully consumed, or a
+ * negative errno on error.
+ */
+static __always_inline int iomap_iter_next(const struct iomap_iter *iter,
+		struct iomap *iomap, struct iomap *srcmap,
+		iomap_iter_begin_fn begin, iomap_iter_end_fn end)
+{
+	int ret = 0;
+
+	if (iomap->length) {
+		if (end) {
+			/*
+			 * Calculate how far the iter was advanced and the
+			 * original length bytes for end().
+			 */
+			ssize_t advanced = iter->pos - iter->iter_start_pos;
+			loff_t len;
+
+			len = iomap_length_trim(iter, iter->iter_start_pos,
+					iter->len + advanced);
+
+			ret = end(iter->inode, iter->iter_start_pos, len,
+					advanced, iter->flags, iomap);
+		}
+		ret = iomap_iter_continue(iter, iomap, srcmap, ret);
+		if (ret <= 0)
+			return ret;
+	}
+
+	ret = begin(iter->inode, iter->pos, iter->len, iter->flags, iomap,
+			srcmap);
+
+	return ret < 0 ? ret : 1;
+}
+
+#define DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT_END(name, begin_fn, end_fn)		\
+int name(const struct iomap_iter *iter, struct iomap *iomap,		\
+		struct iomap *srcmap)					\
+{									\
+	return iomap_iter_next(iter, iomap, srcmap, begin_fn, end_fn);	\
+}
+
+#define DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT(name, begin_fn)				\
+	DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT_END(name, begin_fn, NULL)
+
 /*

2.52.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged, confirmed
Joanne Koong (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the interaction between ->iomap_next() and ->iomap_end(), explaining that iomap_iter() uses ->iomap_next() when available, but falls back to ->iomap_begin()/->iomap_end() if not. The author did not indicate any plans for a fix.
Show original comment 
Have one ->iomap_next() callback instead of ->iomap_begin() and
->iomap_end(). ->iomap_next() finishes the previous mapping if needed,
and produces the next mapping.

Collapsing to a single callback lets a performance-critical caller
inline its iteration loop and pass its ->iomap_next() function as a
compile-time constant, so the compiler can devirtualize that callback
into a direct call instead of an indirect call through a function
pointer.

iomap_iter() uses ->iomap_next() when the filesystem provides that
callback and otherwise falls back to the ->iomap_begin()/->iomap_end()
path, so filesystems can be converted one at a time.

Suggested-by: Christoph Hellwig <hch@lst.de>
Suggested-by: Matthew Wilcox (Oracle) <willy@infradead.org>
Signed-off-by: Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com>

+static inline void
+iomap_dio_simple_finish(struct iomap_iter *iomi, const struct iomap_ops *ops,
+		size_t len, size_t written)
+{
+	if (ops->iomap_next) {
+		iomi->iter_start_pos = iomi->pos;
+		iomi->pos += written;
+		iomi->len -= written;

+		ops->iomap_end(iomi->inode, iomi->pos, len, written,
+			       iomi->flags, &iomi->iomap);
+	}

2.52.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: NEUTRAL
Joanne Koong (author) Needs Work LLM
Author Fengnan Chang pointed out that the ->iomap_next() model causes an extra indirect call and iomap_iter() frames on every I/O for DIO reads on filesystems without a ->iomap_end() work, such as ext4. Author Joanne Koong responded by adding a new flag IOMAP_DIO_NO_IOMAP_END to allow callers to promise there is no ->iomap_end() work and no folio batch, letting iomap_dio_simple() skip the finishing iomap_iter() on both success and error-exit paths.
Show original comment 
From: Fengnan Chang <changfengnan@bytedance.com>

With the ->iomap_next() model, iomap_dio_simple() will run a second,
full iomap_iter() just to finish the range.  For DIO reads on
filesystems with no ->iomap_end() work (ext4, xfs, exfat, ntfs) this is
a no-op, but still costs an extra indirect ->iomap_next() call and the
iomap_iter() frames on every I/O.

Add IOMAP_DIO_NO_IOMAP_END so such callers can promise there is no
->iomap_end() work and no folio batch, letting iomap_dio_simple() skip
the finishing iomap_iter() on both the success and error-exit paths. Opt
in the ext4, xfs, exfat and ntfs direct read paths; filesystems with
real read-path ->iomap_end() work keep the existing behaviour.

Suggested-by: Christoph Hellwig <hch@lst.de>
Signed-off-by: Fengnan Chang <changfengnan@bytedance.com>
Signed-off-by: Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com>

 #define IOMAP_DIO_BOUNCE		(1 << 4)
 
+/*
+ * The caller's ->iomap_next() has no ->iomap_end() work to run and this I/O
+ * does not use a folio batch, so the iomap_dio_simple() fast path may skip the
+ * finishing iomap_iter() call entirely.  Only meaningful on the simple read
+ * fast path; ignored by the generic __iomap_dio_rw() slow path.
+ */
+#define IOMAP_DIO_NO_IOMAP_END		(1 << 5)
+
 ssize_t iomap_dio_rw(struct kiocb *iocb, struct iov_iter *iter,
 		const struct iomap_ops *ops, const struct iomap_dio_ops *dops,
 		unsigned int dio_flags, void *private, size_t done_before);

Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed, added new code to address issue
Joanne Koong (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the xfs iomap_ops implementation, specifically that it was not using the new ->iomap_next() callback as intended. The author explained that each callback is generated with macros that wrap the iomap_iter_next() helper to finish the previous mapping if needed and produce the next one.
Show original comment 
Convert xfs iomap_ops to the new ->iomap_next() callback. Each callback is
generated with the DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT()/DEFINE_IOMAP_ITER_NEXT_END()
macros, which wrap the iomap_iter_next() helper to finish the previous
mapping if needed and produce the next one. No functional changes are
intended.

Reviewed-by: Christoph Hellwig <hch@lst.de>
Signed-off-by: Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com>

>
> >
> > Maybe the striping setup can also be done with a notification.  The
>
> I had interpreted the FUSE_DEV_IOC_STRIPE_OPEN patch as implying that

different.

Last comment 2026-07-23 Re: [PATCH 2/3] iomap: decouple simple direct I/O reads from iomap_dio_rw Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch decouples simple direct I/O reads from the iomap_dio_rw function, making it cleaner and more modular. It introduces a new function __iomap_dio_simple() to handle these reads, which properly accounts for synchronous cases by calling inode_dio_end() even on errors.

Last comment 2026-07-22 Re: [PATCH RFC 00/32] mm/mglru: MGLRU-FG and refault distance support Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces MGLRU-FG and refault distance support to the Linux kernel's memory management system, improving performance by optimizing page replacement decisions. The changes include a new flag format rework, a generic LRU-refs API, and modifications to working set and referenced flags. The patches are currently being reviewed and discussed, with some suggestions for splitting the series into independent chunks to facilitate review and testing.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Kairui Song noted that the refault distance unification contributes little to scan resistance related jobs and none of the benchmarks in the RFC's cover letter benefit much from it, suggesting separating this part out for easier review. Reviewer noted that the performance gain in patch 22 is primarily due to MGLRU-FG changes, and the refault distance improvements contribute a negligible amount. Reviewer suggested dropping the flags unification part first to simplify MGLRU-FG's refs tracking calculations, which would eliminate unnecessary bit shifts in every operation.

Last comment 2026-07-23 Re: [PATCH RFC 00/32] mm/mglru: MGLRU-FG and refault distance support Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces MGLRU-FG (Multi-Granularity Least Recently Used - Fault Generation) and refault distance support, which aims to improve the performance of page faults in Linux by allowing for more efficient handling of page replacements. The patches are designed to work with a new flag format that unifies various flags used in the kernel. However, there is a concern about the order of review and submission, as some developers think it would be better to first land the flag format changes and then submit the MGLRU-FG patches separately. This would allow for concurrent review and potentially speed up the process.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Kairui Song ↳ replying to Joanne Koong Needs Work LLM
Reviewer pointed out that rearranging the flags for MGLRU-FG and removing single-bit operations would simplify the code, but this would require reordering the patches so that the flag unification work comes first.
Show original comment 
Hi,

That's not how it works, though. Without the MGLRU-FG, it takes four
bits for tracking the tier and these two flag bits have special
meanings. After FG, only three bits are needed and these two original
flag bits are just the lower two bits of a three-bit counter now. So,
there is an advantage to rearranging them together and removing the
single-bit (flag) operations to always use them as a whole. Before
that, that conversion will actual make thing more ugly.

BTW will there be any feature change with your splitting plan? If
there is no feature change, that should be easy to review.

Patches Submitted (1)
Last comment 2026-07-22 [PATCH v2 0/4] mm: fix reclaim storms in defrag_mode 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm: page_alloc: __GFP_FS lockdep annotation for direct compaction
  • [2/4] mm: compaction: support non-movable compaction for pageblock requests Neutral Last comment 2026-07-22 Gregory Price
  • [3/4] mm: page_alloc: move capture_control to the page allocator Neutral Last comment 2026-07-22 Gregory Price
  • [4/4] mm: page_alloc: fix non-movable reclaim storm in defrag_mode Neutral Last comment 2026-07-22 Brendan Jackman Andrew Morton
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses reclaim storms in defrag_mode by allowing non-movable requests to participate in pageblock production during direct reclaim and compaction, which helps prevent pressure spikes and swap storms. The patches make adjustments to the allocator and compaction code to enable this behavior, including changes to capture control and migration source scan restrictions. The approach taken is to modify the slowpath of the allocator to invoke direct reclaim and compaction with pageblock_order when non-movable requests are involved. This has been tested in production against affected workloads for several weeks and restores OOM kill rates to baseline levels.

Johannes Weiner Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about the interface being too limited and premature, suggesting that it should be designed to accommodate future features like limits, hierarchies, and demotion rules.
Show original comment 
Hello Youngjun,

On Thu, Jul 23, 2026 at 08:39:22PM +0900, Youngjun Park wrote:
> On Wed, Jul 22, 2026 at 09:14:10AM -0400, Johannes Weiner wrote:
> 
> Hello Johannes!
> 
> > Zswap is a first-order swap destination with writeback semantics. The
> > way the discussion around zswap has been going in this thread is
> > disappointing, and I don't feel comfortable adding permanent user
> > interfaces on this basis.
> 
> I have given this a lot of thought. First, could you clarify exactly which
> part of the discussion or consensus makes you hesitate? Understanding this
> will help me re-evaluate my proposal.
> 
> To make sure we are on the same page, I would like to share my thoughts and
> vision for the present and future of the tier interface semantics for your
> further consideration.
> 
> The interface should provide the swap amount allocated to a tier and allow
> the use of the swap device defined by that tier via swap.tiers.max.
> Currently, it would only support 0 and 'max' (essentially on/off for
> explicit usage). Auto-demotion is planned for the future, and specifying
> exact capacity limits is still to be determined. (I have also reviewed
> potential interface collisions and duplications based on Yosry's guidance.)

This is the part that makes me uncomfortable. The interface as
proposed now is just a small subset of what should eventually be "swap
tiers" - with limits, hierarchies and demotion rules. It seems to be
an unordered blacklist/whitelist for swapfiles right now.

And how the final form looks like depends on the indirection layer.

As Chris says upthread, virtual swap will be a whole new world. But
unfortunately interfaces are permanent. So I'm sorry, but it's
premature to start tying us to an ABI direction here.

> > We will not be merging a memcg swap tier interface until the swap side
> > has a story for indirection and backend migration.
> 
> Regarding this point, is your position that an indirect layer (I see
> this is virtualized swap) must be implemented first?

It is implemented. There have been patches floating around since the
beginning of 2025. Initial proposals date back to 2023.

But we need settled consensus before we can start adding interfaces
that are supposed to control that very layer long-term.
Signals: premature, limited

This patch adds support for batch writeback in the zswap shrink_memcg() function, ensuring consistency with other reclaimers like direct reclaim and cgroup limit reclaim.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants

Yosry Ahmed ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Yosry Ahmed suggested keeping the writeback non-batched and doing the batching change separately due to potential synchronous latency issues, and recommended measuring performance impact before making changes.
Show original comment 
I suggested that we keep the writeback here without batching and do
that change separately, mainly out of abundance of caution as
writeback is done synchronously here so the extra latency could be
problematic. I think we probably want to measure the performance
impact of that separately.

batching here.

What do you think?

Johannes Weiner (author) ↳ replying to Yosry Ahmed Neutral LLM
Author addressed Yosry Ahmed's concern about the arbitrary global limit of 64 vs cgroup limit of 1, explaining that batching is consistent with direct reclaim and cgroup limit reclaim, but prefers to start with a clean and consistent version rather than introducing an inconsistency.
Show original comment 
It's not an unknown, right? We know this works for direct reclaimers,
cgroup limit reclaim e.g., and what the latency implications are.

Because of how reclaim works, we also know it'll call zswap_store() in
batches of SWAP_CLUSTER_MAX. If we don't batch here, they're likely to
each call shrink_memcg() once we're at the limit - while still risking
rejections due to compressibility differences.

My worry is that if we start with an inconsistency, we'll be stuck
with it for a long time.

I'd rather start with the clean, consistent version. Dial it back only
if we have data to justfiy the complication that we can put into a
comment and the changelog that outlines why exactly it's different.

> > > The same page can be swapped out once, and swap in multiple times :)
> > >
> > > Unlike zswap, physical swap in is not necessarily exclusive. You load
> > > the page in memory, but if the swapfile is not full, etc. etc. you
> > > don't invalidate the copy of the data on the swapfile. At reclaim
> > > time, we notice the page is not dirty, so we just skip (z)swapout.

> > Oh right, I forgot about that, thanks.

> > I still don't fully understand why pswpin is significantly increased
> > with batching here. I would hope that with less LRU inversion we end
> > up with less disk swapin. Probably the test access patterns are just
> > too random compared to real workloads?
>
> Maybe more time in zswap for those tail entries left behind with the
> smaller batch?
>
> That's a 32 entry window that could zswpin with the smaller batch but
> pswpin with the larger one.

Oh I was talking about going from no batching to batch=32. pspwin
increased by 387,400 (~30%). We are writing back 272,880 more pages
but we also have 209,837 less store rejections. So overall ~ 63,043
more pages should end up on disk, which doesn't explain the increase
in pspwin.

The only explanation I can think of is that the test is just randomly
accessing memory, so it ends up accessing colder memory that we moved
to disk more than hotter memory that we kept in zswap. IOW, the access
patterns do not conform to a "normal" workload that benefits from the
LRU.

Re: [RFC PATCH v3 2/4] mm: distinguish large folio swap allocation failures Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-07-23 Re: [PATCH] mm: mempolicy: fix automatic numa balancing for shmem Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a problem with automatic NUMA balancing for shared memory (shmem) by fixing the default policy used when no explicit policy is set. The issue arises from silently inherited policies on boot, which can be confusing for users and lead to unexpected behavior. To resolve this, the patch proposes setting a default global policy that includes MPOL_LOCAL and additional flags to ensure consistent NUMA balancing.

8 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory Price ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Gregory Price expressed concern about the patch's impact on users, specifically noting that silently inherited policies (MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON) will be confusing and suggested adding documentation to clarify the default global policy as MPOL_LOCAL + (MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON)
Show original comment 
I lean towards Johannes' stance here that consistency is better, and if
KVM has a known-poor interaction with numa balancing, maybe KVM should
simply set a default policy (when none are present) to avoid this.

That said, this is painful because MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON are already
a silently inherited policy on boot (added during init).  This will be
confusing for users.

This is not documented anywhere.  Maybe this change should come with a
docs addition that says the default global policy is ACTUALLY:

   MPOL_LOCAL + (MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON)

Instead of just MPOL_LOCAL and letting that silent implementation
detail bite people.

~Gregory
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes, concerns about user experience
Johannes Weiner ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Johannes Weiner pointed out that the documentation does not clearly state the default global policy for numa balancing, specifically mentioning that MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON are internal flags and should not be silently inherited on boot. He suggested adding a doc addition to clarify this.
Show original comment 
Hm, where do you see "Instead of just MPOL_LOCAL"?

Documentation/filesystems/tmpfs.rst says it defaults to "default" and
points me to set_mempolicy(2). That in turn in general makes little
mention of numa balancing and doesn't mention MPOL_F_MOF/MPOL_F_MORON.
In fact these are documented as internal flags.

The numa_balancing entry in
Documentation/admin-guide/sysctl/kernel.rst however DOES say plainly
that enabling this feature will sample and migrate mapped process
memory to where it's used, and that this has a performance penalty.

It seems there is a general lack of documented interactions between
user-requested policies and the system-wide numa-balancing behavior.
View on lore ↗
Signals: lack of documentation, silent implementation detail
Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING is an internal flag, essentially a shorthand for (F_MOF | F_MORON), and explained its usage as 'internal' and generated by passing this flag. Author acknowledged Johannes' feedback about the silent implementation detail of MPOL_F_MOF / MPOL_F_MORON being inherited on boot, and suggested setting MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING in default/preferred policies to make it clear that numa balancing is enabled by default. The author proposes documenting this behavior.
Show original comment 
They're "internal" and generated by passing MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING...
which is basically shorthand for "use (F_MOF | F_MORON)".  There is
no other user of MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING.

The internal/external state of uapi/linux/mempolicy.h is frustrating
to say the last.

---

Agreed, that's basically what i'm pointing out.

This is what most think they're getting:

    static struct mempolicy default_policy = {
        .refcnt = ATOMIC_INIT(1), /* never free it */
        .mode = MPOL_LOCAL,
    };

This is what they're actually getting:

    struct mempolicy *get_task_policy(struct task_struct *p)
    {
        ...
                pol = &preferred_node_policy[node];
                /* preferred_node_policy is not initialised early in boot */
                if (pol->mode)
                        return pol;
    }

    void __init numa_policy_init(void)
    {
    ...
            for_each_node(nid) {
                preferred_node_policy[nid] = (struct mempolicy) {
                    .refcnt = ATOMIC_INIT(1),
                    .mode = MPOL_PREFERRED,
                    .flags = MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON,
                    .nodes = nodemask_of_node(nid),
            };
        }
    }

So the default global mempolicy is effectively:

    (MPOL_PREFERRED | (MPOL_F_MOF | MPOL_F_MORON)

which is just MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING without MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING.

The user can't actually see that - because the MOF/MORON flags get
stripped out when it's queried (because they're internal only).

Maybe we should just set MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING in the default/preferred
policies and document that the global preferred policy default-enables
MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING - and that if a user (KVM) doesn't want to be
affected by numa balancing it needs to set a policy to disable it.

~Gregory
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged technical detail, explained complexity, acknowledged a fix is needed
Gregory Price (author) Positive LLM
Author acknowledged that Johannes' fix is correct and there are no additional changes required to address the issue of KVM interacting poorly with numa balancing, confirming that users enabling numa balancing while running KVM will get the expected performance hit.
Show original comment 
Walking this all back:

  - MPOL_DEFAULT is the default policy
  - numa_balancing=on means enable NUMA_BALANCING on the defaul policy
  - shmem was broken and did not respect this

Johannes' fix is correct, and there are no additional changes required.

Users enabling numa balancing while running KVM with shared regions are
not getting what they're asking for presently - that is a bug.

They should get the performance hit they're asking for (and then either
turn off numa balancing or set an explicit mempolicy for those tasks /
memfd's).

~Gregory
